«, or with rin.


200 THE YORUBA-SPEAKING PEOPLES.

The personal pronouns are made reflexive by
adding ti-kara {1% of, or belonging to; ara, body,
form) : —

Tikara 'mi, Myself. Tikara 'wa. Ourselves.

By placing na (the same that) after a personal
pronoun the particularising power is increased. It
denotes the exclusion of any other person, and answers
to " myself," " himself," &c. :—

Emi nQf I myself. Awa na, We ourselves.

(2) Relative pronouns.

There is only one, viz.: ti, which means who,
whom, which, that.

In the nominative case the personal pronoun to
which it relates is always placed after it, as
lya ti ofe ^mi, " the mother who loves me," literally,
" the mother who she loves me." In the objective
case the relative pronoun is governed by the verb, and
the personal pronoun is not required. Thus lya ti
emi f By " the mother whom I love."

(3) Demonstrative pronouns.
These are : —

Eyt'l/h ^Vh or yh This. Wonyi, These.

Ei/iniy eni, nt, or na, That. Wonni, Those.

Eyiyiy eyi, or yi, seems to mean literally **' the thing
taken," and Eyini to be composed of eyiy and eni,
one. TFoTi, is an abbreviation of aworiy they.

The adverbial demonstrative pronouns of time,
place, and manner are expressed by : —

Nighana, then, at that time (ni, to be ; igha, time; na, that).
Nihin-yi, here, in this place {niha, locality, place ; eyi, this).


LANGUAGE.


201


Nihe-na^ there, in that place (ni^e, in or at a plac3 ; m<, that).
Baiy thus, in this manner (is perhaps a contraction of ha-eyi, to meet
this).

(4) Interrogative pronouns.

Tani, Who.
EvoOj Which.

The possessive cases are made by prefixing <i,
" belonging to " : —


Nouns.

Nouns are formed in the following ways : —

(1) From the verb, with the prefix i. This prefix
seems to convey the notion of things regarded collec-
tively, or in the abstract.


//"e, love, willingness,

/<7a, height, loftiness,

Ika^ computation, reckoning,

Iriy a seeing, sight,

Iloy the act of going,

Ita, pain, smart,

/cfu, blackness,

Ise, cookery,

IkpamOf the act of hiding,

Ikparty erasure,


fropa f€y to love.

ga^ to be high, or tall.
Ara, to count.
ri^ to see.
/o, to go.
to, to sting.
du, to be black.
8€, to prepare food.
kpamOf to hide.
kpare, to rub out.


(2) From the verb, with the prefix a. This prefix
seems to limit the abstract notion of the verb and
make it more concrete. Properly speaking, it seems
to carry with it a suggestion of passiveness, or of
individuality of things rather than of beings, but
noims expressing personality are nevertheless some-


202 THE YORUBA-SPEAKING PEOPLES.

times formed with this prefix. A is occasionally
changed to e.

AfCj pleasure, a state of loving, from/«, to love.

Aga^ a chair, elevated seat, , „ ga^ to be high.

Aka^ a storehouse, ,, X:a, to count.

Aloj departure, „ /<?, to go.

Ata^ pepper, capsicum, „ <a, to sting.

Eloj utensil, j, lo, to use.

Edu, cinder, „ du, to be black.

Ela, splinter, chip, „ la, to split, cleave.

AkpejOf fisherman, „ kpejoj to fish.

Afonnu, boaster, „ fonnu, to boast.

(3) From the verb, with the prefix o. This prefix
is the personal pronoun o, he, she, or it, and conveys
the idea of distinct or active personality. It gives
the meaning of "one who," or " that which."

Obt, parent (one who has borne), from hi, to bear, beget.

ObOf nurse, „ bo, to feed, maintain.

Oku, corpse, „ ku, to die.

Olu, borer, gimlet (that which bores), „ In, to perforate.

Omi, water (that which is swallowed), „ mi, to swallow.

Oka, ring (that which is coiled) „ ka, to coil.

Oyun, pregnancy, „ yun, to be pregnant.

Obiriy woman; olconri, man; ofe, parrot; oforo,
squirrel ; ogboj wild cat ; okete, bandicoot ; oloyo,
monkey ; oivivn, owl ; otuo, child ; ofea, king.

(4) By joining a verb and a noun.

Arin-ko, chance, from arin, middle, midst, and ko, to meet.

Ajin-ta, kidnapper, ,, ajin, darkness, depth of night, and ta, to

pass from one place to another.
Atiko, teaching, ,, ati, purpose, intention, and ko, to teach.

Ati-lo^ departure, „ ati, and lo, to go.

(5) From the verb, by reduplication. This mode is


LANGUAGE. 203

common to the other cognate languages, and is pro-
bably primitive.

(a) Eegular reduplication : —

Fofo^ foam, from/o, to float,

iVent, owner, „ ni, to possess.

i?irf, sight, „ n', to see.

Konrinkonrin, singer „ konrin^ to sing.

Ghenaghena^ carpenter, „ gbena^ to canrc, work in wood.

(b) Irregular reduplication : —

Mmu, something to be drunkt from muy to drink.
TitOj snfliciency, „ to, to be enough.

Gtga, height, „ ga, to be tall.

Jija^ fight, battle, „ ja, to fight.

Jijin, depth, „ ji, to be deep.

(6) By prefixing the personal pronoun o (he, she,
or it) to a verb of possession, as : —

Onija, antagonist, fighter, from nija (ni ija)y to have strife.
Onijo, dancer, „ nijo (ni ijo), to have a dance.

OnidajOf judge „ nidajo (m idajo), to have

judgment.

Where the ni of the verb of possession has been
euphonically changed to Zi, or Z, the initial vowel of
the original noun becomes the prefix of the noun of
possession. Thus : —

Alafose, diviner, from lafose {ni-afose^ to have divination.

Aladugbo, neighbour, „ ladugho (ni-adugbo), to have vicinity.

Elekun, mourner, „ lehcn (ni-ekun), to have grief.

Elegan, slanderer, „ legan {ni-egan)^ to have slander,

OlowOi rich man, „ lowo (ni-owo), to have riches.

0/q/b, loser, „ /q/b, (ni-^fo), to have loss.

We may here show the successive changes by which


204 THE YORUBA'SPEAKING PEOPLES.

the noun of possession is formed from the primitive
monosyllabic verb : —

Monosyllabic Verb. Noun.

2?u, to carry. Eru^ burden.

Foy to be empty. Ofo^ emptinesSi loss.

Dat to make. Ida, act of making.

Verb of Possession. Noun of Possession.

Leru {ni-eru), to have a burden. Eleru, carrier.

Lo/o (ni-ofo), to have loss. OlofOf loser.

NidOf to have the making. Onida^ maker.

(7) By joining two nouns, as : —

Ebado, river-side, from eba, brink, edge, and odo, river, brook.

Eyinju, oye-ball, „ eyin, egg, and oju, eye.

Ibqji, grave, „ ibi, place, and oji, shade, ghost.

InnajOy travel, „ tVin, a walk, and ajo^ journey.

(8) By prefixing a, "not," to nouns commencing
with i. This gives the notion of a negative quality
in the abstract. Thus : —

AifCt unwillingness. Ailo, inutility.

From the foregoing it will be seen that all nouns
begin with a vowel, except those formed by re-
duplication from a verb. A few nouns may be found
which appear to be exceptions to this rule, but on
investigation they are found to be either : —

(a) Nouns of foreign importation, such as gabas,
east, a Hausa word, or

(b) Nouns whose vowel-prefix has been dropped.

Of the latter, we find feq/i, a grave ; balogun^ war-
chief ; baluwe^ bath-room ; bode, custom-house ; and,
as we as often hear iboji as boji^ and ibalogun as balo^


LANGUAGE. 205

gun^ we conclude that hode^ and a few other similar
words, originally had the vowel-prefix.

Conversely, all words beginning with a vowel are
nouns. A few words used as adjectives, adverbs,
or conjunctions, appear at first sight to be exceptions,
but they are all really nouns. Thus : —

Ahiye, used as an adjective, ^^ winged," really means '^ something
possessing wings.** {Ahiy something possessing).

Ahila^ used to express "striped," similarly means "something
possessing stripes.'*

Igbana, used to express "then,** is really igba (time) and na
(that).

Okpo lokpo, used as an adverb, " plentifully,*' is a reduplication of
the noun okpo, plenty, and means literally " plenty-plenty."

Nouns are not declined. The noun in the nomina-
tive, or subjective, case is placed before the verb, and,
in the objective, after it. The genitive is either
expressed, as in Ewe, by position, as tie okonri, house
(of the) man, or by means of ti, belonging to, as
He ti okonriy house of the man.

The plural is formed by placing the demonstrative
pronoun wonyi (these), or wonni (those), after the
noun, except when a number is used, in which case
the number itself, placed after the noun, sufficiently
indicates the plural.

There is, properly speaking, no gender in Yoruba,
as is shown by the same word serving for he, she, or
it. In the case of human beings, infants excepted,
sex is indicated by the nouns ohonri (man) and ohiri
(woman). Thus : —

OmO'konrif son. Omo-biriy daughter.

In the case of all other living creatures, humau


206 THE YORUBA'SPEAKING PEOPLES.

infants included, it is indicated by the words ako {a
male) and aho (a female), as : —

Ako-malUf bull. Aho'elede^ boar.

Abo'tnalu^ cow. Aho-eledej sow.

Ako and aho^ when applied to infants, st^nd alone,
without being joined to a noim meaning child or
infant.

Adjectives.

Adjectives are expressed in various ways.
(1.) By verbs, as : —

Gay to be tall. TFb, to be bent.

Mo, to be clean. Ro, to be soft.

Ft, to be tough. Te, to be flat.

These adjectival verbs admit of comparison, and
are the only words qualifying nouns that can be
compared. The comparative is formed by adding the
verb ju (to surpass, be more than) ; and the super-
lative by adding the verb lo (to leave, depart), to the
comparative. The comparative is thus expressed by
a compound verb of Class III., and the superlative
by one of Class V. Example : —

Ga, to be tall.

Ga-ju, to be taller (to be tall, to surpass).

Ga-ju lo, to be tallest (to be tall, to surpass, to leave).

(2) By verbs of possession, as : —

Nikpara (ni-tkpara), to have harm, expresses harmful.

Ntbo (ni'tbo), to have breadth, „ wide.

Nikpa (ni'ikpa)y to have strength, „ powerful.

Laiya (ni-aiya), to have courage, „ brave.


LANGUAGE. 207

These can also be used in the negative. Thus : —

Lfti-nikparaf not to have harm, expresses harmless.

Lai-niboj not to have breadth, „ narrow.

Lai-nikpa^ not to have strength, „ powerless.

(3) By nouns with the negative particle ai (no, not),
or lai (not being), prefixed. Thus : —

Aidaba (at idaba, no hope) can be used to express hopeless,
^ti^dona (at t^^o/ia, no warmth) „ „ cold.

Zat daju {lai idajuj not being

certainty) ,, ,, uncertain.

Laidon (lai idon, not being

sweetness) „ ,, unpleasant.

The only adjectives proper are those formed by
reduplication from a verb, ordinarily an adjectival
verb. Examples : —

Wiwo, bent, crooked, from wo^ to be bent.

Tito, straight, „ to, to be straight.

Lile, hard, „ le, to be hard.

Lele, pliant, „ Uj to be pliant.

Giga, high, tall, „ ga, to be high or tall.

Dudu, black, „ duy to be black.

Kvkuru, short, „ htnt, to be short.

Euruj disordered, „ nt, to stir up, mingle.

Jvju^ confused, „ jm, to surpass, differ.


Adverbs.

Adverbs are usually expressed by verbs, nouns,
adjectives, or compounds of these, used adverbially.
The word used as an adverb is always placed after
the verb or objective noun. Examples : —

Awhile, for a time, by osa, time, interval of time.

Before (sooner), „ sin, to lead the way.


208
